<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We construct a closed connected orientable smooth three-manifold that admits a smooth Riemannian metric without conjugate points but admits no smooth Riemannian metric of nonpositive sectional curvature.

- **C1** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There exists a closed connected orientable smooth three-manifold $M$ with a $C^\infty$ Riemannian metric without conjugate points, such that $M$ admits no $C^\infty$ Riemannian metric whose sectional curvature is everywhere at most zero.
  - Explanation: The constructed manifold separates existence of a metric without conjugate points from existence of a smooth nonpositively curved metric.
- **C2** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-no-cat0-action)): The fundamental group $\Gamma=\pi_1(M)$ of the closed three-manifold defined by <a href="#eq:torus-gluing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:torus-gluing">[eq:torus-gluing]</a> admits no proper cocompact isometric action on a nonempty proper $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space.
  - Explanation: The prescribed gluing forces incompatible translation-vector orthogonality conditions under the hypothesized geometric action.
- **C3** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-no-local-cat0)): The manifold $M$ admits no locally $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ length metric inducing its manifold topology.
  - Explanation: A topology-compatible locally nonpositively curved length metric would yield the forbidden action on the universal cover.
- **C4** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-no-npc)): The closed smooth three-manifold $M$ defined by <a href="#eq:torus-gluing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:torus-gluing">[eq:torus-gluing]</a> admits no smooth Riemannian metric of nonpositive sectional curvature.
  - Explanation: A smooth nonpositively curved metric would contradict the length-metric obstruction.
- **C5** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-cometric-bounds)): There are constants $\delta_0>0$ and $C<\infty$, depending only on the fixed function $\chi$, such that for $0<\delta\le\delta_0$ the path <a href="#eq:cometric-path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cometric-path">[eq:cometric-path]</a> satisfies, in the original coordinates and in either endpoint coordinate system, $$\begin{gathered} \frac19\operatorname{Id}\preceq P\preceq18\operatorname{Id}, \qquad \frac1{18}\operatorname{Id}\preceq Q\preceq9\operatorname{Id}, \qquad \\|P'\\|\le C\delta, \label{eq:cometric-uniform}\\ -P''\succeq0, \qquad \|P'p\|^2\le C\delta\,p^{\mathsf T}(-P'')p \quad\text{for every }p\in\mathbb R^2. \label{eq:cometric-domination} \end{gathered}$$ The constants do not depend on $B_1$ or $B_2$.
  - Explanation: The cometric remains uniformly positive definite and concave, with derivative domination in both endpoint frames.
- **C6** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-tangential-invariance)): The integrand defining $I_J^\perp(V)$ is the index integrand of $V^\perp=V-q_VT$. Consequently, for every smooth real function $h$, $$I_J^\perp(V+hT)=I_J^\perp(V)$$ provided both fields have the finite integrals required in <a href="#eq:normal-index-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:normal-index-definition">[eq:normal-index-definition]</a>. This invariance holds pointwise, without any condition on $h$ at finite endpoints.
  - Explanation: Tangential changes preserve the perpendicular index integrand even with unrestricted finite endpoint values.
- **C7** (theorem, [source](#stmt-lem-coordinate-index)): For a smooth field $V=(w,z)$ on $[a,c]$, $$\label{eq:coordinate-index} I_{[a,c]}(V)=E_{[a,c]}(V) -\bigl[\langle T,\Gamma(V,V)\rangle\bigr]_a^c,$$ where $\Gamma$ denotes the Christoffel symbols in the chosen product coordinates and brackets mean final value minus initial value. In the inward coordinates at either neck boundary, if $w=0$ at that boundary, then $$\label{eq:boundary-christoffel} \Gamma(V,V)=\tfrac\kappa2 z_1^2\partial_\tau.$$ Thus the endpoint contribution in <a href="#eq:coordinate-index" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coordinate-index">[eq:coordinate-index]</a> is nonnegative at an entry into or exit from the open neck, provided the interval component of the field vanishes there.
  - Explanation: The coordinate energy differs from the intrinsic index by an endpoint correction with a favorable boundary sign under the stated field condition.
- **C8** (classification, [source](#stmt-lem-neck-passages)): Every finite endpoint of a neck passage is transverse to the boundary torus. Exactly one of the following alternatives holds.* 1. *The passage is the whole real line and $t$ is constant.* 2. *The interval component $v=\dot t$ never vanishes. The passage is either a finite crossing between the two different boundary tori, or a half-infinite interval approaching an interior equilibrium height.* 3. *After choosing the coordinates from one boundary and translating the geodesic parameter, the passage has closure $[0,L]$, begins and ends at that boundary, and has a unique turn at $s=L/2$. In these coordinates $$\begin{gathered} t(0)=t(L)=0,\qquad v(0)=-v(L)=v_0>0,\qquad t_*=t(L/2)\in(0,1),\label{eq:turn-data}\\ t(L-s)=t(s),\qquad v(L-s)=-v(s),\qquad 0<F'(t)\le\kappa p_1^2\quad(0\le t\le t_*),\label{eq:turn-concavity}\\ p_1\ne0,\qquad 0<v_0^2=F(t_*)-F(0)\le\kappa p_1^2. \label{eq:turn-incidence} \end{gathered}$$* *An interior equilibrium height $t_\infty$ in the second alternative satisfies $F(t_\infty)=1$ and $F'(t_\infty)=0$; the velocity may tend to zero there. A geodesic tangent to a boundary torus at a finite time does not enter the open neck at that time.
  - Explanation: All entire neck visits fall into constant-height, monotone, or single-turn alternatives; boundary grazing does not enter the open neck.
- **C9** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-elementary-passages)): Let $V$ be the restriction of a smooth compactly supported field along $\gamma$ to an entire neck passage $J$. If $J=\mathbb R$, or $v$ never vanishes on $J$, or the passage is a turn contained in one end portion $0\le\tau\le1/4$, then $$I_J^\perp(V)\ge0.$$
  - Explanation: Entire visits that need no turning estimate have nonnegative perpendicular index for restrictions of compactly supported fields.
- **C10** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-turning-gauge)): Consider a turning passage with data <a href="#eq:turn-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:turn-data">[eq:turn-data]</a>– <a href="#eq:turn-incidence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:turn-incidence">[eq:turn-incidence]</a>. For every smooth field $V$ on $[0,L]$, there is a tangentially equivalent smooth field, denoted again by $V=(w,z)$, such that $$w(0)=w(L)=0,\qquad \langle V,T\rangle\ \text{is affine in }s.$$ Put $\Delta z=z(L)-z(0)$, $e=(1,0)^{\mathsf T}$, and $\mu=\kappa v_0/4>0$. For this field, $$\begin{aligned} I_{[0,L]}^\perp(V) &=E_{[0,L]}(V)+\frac{\kappa v_0}{2} \bigl(z_1(0)^2+z_1(L)^2\bigr) -\frac{(p^{\mathsf T}\Delta z)^2}{L} \label{eq:turning-index-identity}\\ &\ge E_{[0,L]}(V)+\mu(e^{\mathsf T}\Delta z)^2 -\frac{(p^{\mathsf T}\Delta z)^2}{L}. \label{eq:turning-index-reduction} \end{aligned}$$ The same left side is the normal index of the original field.
  - Explanation: A tangential gauge makes the endpoint interval components vanish while making the tangential coefficient affine, isolating a boundary penalty.
- **C11** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-turning-index)): For all sufficiently small positive $\delta$, every returning geodesic passage of the constructed neck has nonnegative perpendicular index form $I^\perp(V)$ for every smooth field $V$ along the closed passage, with no restriction on its endpoint values. A single choice of $\delta$ works for both boundary components and all unit-speed returning geodesics.
  - Explanation: A common small construction parameter controls returning visits through either end with arbitrary endpoint fields.
- **C12** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-turning-time)): For every returning passage, $$\label{eq:turning-chord} \int_0^L v(s)^2\,ds\le \frac{Lv_0^2}{3}.$$ If $t_*>1/4$, then $$\label{eq:turning-time-margin} 4v_0\le\beta\kappa p_1^2L, \qquad \beta=\frac{1+\exp(-9/26)}{2}<1.$$
  - Explanation: Convexity of the normal velocity bounds its squared integral, and leaving the endpoint collar gives a strict timing margin.
- **C13** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-turning-duality)): For every constant covector $q$ and every gauged field as above, $$\label{eq:turning-dual-cs} \|q^\top X\|^2\le S(q,q)E.$$ For each real $\alpha$, the boundary penalty gives the further bound $$\label{eq:turning-boundary-cs} \|p^\top X\|^2\le \left(S(p-\alpha e,p-\alpha e)+\frac{\alpha^2}{\mu}\right) \left(E+\mu(e^\top X)^2\right).$$ The smallest first factor in this inequality is $$\label{eq:turning-minimum} S(p,p)-\frac{S(p,e)^2}{S(e,e)+\mu^{-1}}.$$
  - Explanation: Hilbert-space duality controls displacement by coordinate energy; quadratic minimization incorporates the boundary penalty.
- **C14** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-turning-s-bounds)): There are constants $C_e,C_m>0$, uniform for sufficiently small $\delta>0$, such that every returning passage satisfies $$\begin{aligned} S(p,p)&\le L(1+v_0^2),\label{eq:turning-Spp}\\ S(e,e)&\le C_e L,\label{eq:turning-See}\\ \|S(p,e)-Lp_1\|&\le C_m\delta L\|p_1\|.\label{eq:turning-Spe} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: The momentum, boundary covector, and mixed dual terms admit uniform passage bounds.
- **C15** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-passage-index)): Choose $\delta>0$ to satisfy Lemma <a href="#lem:cometric-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cometric-bounds">5</a> and <a href="#eq:turning-delta-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:turning-delta-choice">[eq:turning-delta-choice]</a>, and then choose the boundary periods as in Section <a href="#sec:cometric" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cometric">3</a>. Let $\gamma$ be any complete unit-speed geodesic of the resulting closed manifold. For every connected component $J$ of its visit to the open neck and every smooth compactly supported field $V$ along $\gamma$, one has $$I_J^\perp(V)\ge0,\qquad I_J(V)\ge0.$$
  - Explanation: The shared parameter choice makes both intrinsic index forms nonnegative on every entire neck visit.
- **C16** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-global-assembly)): Let $(M,g)$ be a complete smooth Riemannian manifold and let $U\subset M$ be open. Suppose that $g$ has nonpositive sectional curvature at every point of $M\setminus U$. Assume also that, for every complete unit-speed geodesic $\gamma:\mathbb R\to M$, every connected component $J$ of $\gamma^{-1}(U)$, and every smooth compactly supported field $V$ along $\gamma$, the intrinsic index form satisfies $$I_J(V)\geq 0.$$ Then $I_{\mathbb R}(V)\geq0$ for every such $\gamma$ and $V$. The same conclusion follows if the passage hypothesis is given in the stronger form $I_J^\perp(V)\geq0$.
  - Explanation: Absolute integrability allows countable addition of original passage integrals and the nonnegative exterior contribution.
- **C17** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-lem-index-criterion)): Let $(M,g)$ be a complete smooth Riemannian manifold. If $I_{\mathbb R}(V)\geq0$ for every smooth compactly supported field along every complete unit-speed geodesic, then $g$ has no conjugate points.
  - Explanation: A truncated Jacobi field and its endpoint derivative jump contradict global index nonnegativity if conjugate points exist.

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-cometric-bounds",
        "exact_quote": "There are constants $\\delta_0>0$ and $C<\\infty$, depending only on the fixed function $\\chi$, such that for $0<\\delta\\le\\delta_0$ the path <a href=\"#eq:cometric-path\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:cometric-path\">[eq:cometric-path]</a> satisfies, in the original coordinates and in either endpoint coordinate system, $$\\begin{gathered}\n \\frac19\\operatorname{Id}\\preceq P\\preceq18\\operatorname{Id},\n \\qquad\n \\frac1{18}\\operatorname{Id}\\preceq Q\\preceq9\\operatorname{Id},\n \\qquad \\|P'\\|\\le C\\delta,\n \\label{eq:cometric-uniform}\\\\\n -P''\\succeq0,\n \\qquad |P'p|^2\\le C\\delta\\,p^{\\mathsf T}(-P'')p\n \\quad\\text{for every }p\\in\\mathbb R^2.\n \\label{eq:cometric-domination}\n\\end{gathered}$$ The constants do not depend on $B_1$ or $B_2$.",
        "source_id": "PM-358.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-cometric-bounds",

          "source_id": "PM-358.1"
        }

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-tangential-invariance",
        "exact_quote": "The integrand defining $I_J^\\perp(V)$ is the index integrand of $V^\\perp=V-q_VT$. Consequently, for every smooth real function $h$, $$I_J^\\perp(V+hT)=I_J^\\perp(V)$$ provided both fields have the finite integrals required in <a href=\"#eq:normal-index-definition\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:normal-index-definition\">[eq:normal-index-definition]</a>.       },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-coordinate-index",
        "exact_quote": "For a smooth field $V=(w,z)$ on $[a,c]$, $$\\label{eq:coordinate-index}\n I_{[a,c]}(V)=E_{[a,c]}(V)\n       -\\bigl[\\langle T,\\Gamma(V,V)\\rangle\\bigr]_a^c,$$ where $\\Gamma$ denotes the Christoffel symbols in the chosen product coordinates and brackets mean final value minus initial value. In the inward coordinates at either neck boundary, if $w=0$ at that boundary, then $$\\label{eq:boundary-christoffel}\n \\Gamma(V,V)=\\tfrac\\kappa2 z_1^2\\partial_\\tau.$$ Thus the endpoint contribution in <a href=\"#eq:coordinate-index\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:coordinate-index\">[eq:coordinate-index]</a> is nonnegative at an entry into or exit from the open neck, provided the interval component of the field vanishes there.",
        "source_id": "PM-358.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-coordinate-index",

In the inward coordinates at either neck boundary, if $w=0$ at that boundary, then $$\\label{eq:boundary-christoffel}\n \\Gamma(V,V)=\\tfrac\\kappa2 z_1^2\\partial_\\tau.$$ Thus the endpoint contribution in <a href=\"#eq:coordinate-index\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:coordinate-index\">[eq:coordinate-index]</a> is nonnegative at an entry into or exit from the open neck, provided the interval component of the field vanishes there.",
          "source_id": "PM-358.1"
        }
      ],

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-neck-passages",
        "exact_quote": "Every finite endpoint of a neck passage is transverse to the boundary torus. Exactly one of the following alternatives holds.*\n\n1.  *The passage is the whole real line and $t$ is constant.*\n\n2.  *The interval component $v=\\dot t$ never vanishes. The passage is either a finite crossing between the two different boundary tori, or a half-infinite interval approaching an interior equilibrium height.*\n\n\n\n\n3.  *After choosing the coordinates from one boundary and translating the geodesic parameter, the passage has closure $[0,L]$, begins and ends at that boundary, and has a unique turn at $s=L/2$. In these coordinates $$\\begin{gathered}\n     t(0)=t(L)=0,\\qquad\n     v(0)=-v(L)=v_0>0,\\qquad t_*=t(L/2)\\in(0,1),\\label{eq:turn-data}\\\\\n     t(L-s)=t(s),\\qquad v(L-s)=-v(s),\\qquad\n     0<F'(t)\\le\\kappa p_1^2\\quad(0\\le t\\le t_*),\\label{eq:turn-concavity}\\\\\n     p_1\\ne0,\\qquad 0<v_0^2=F(t_*)-F(0)\\le\\kappa p_1^2.\n     \\label{eq:turn-incidence}\n    \\end{gathered}$$*\n\n*An interior equilibrium height $t_\\infty$ in the second alternative satisfies $F(t_\\infty)=1$ and $F'(t_\\infty)=0$; the velocity may tend to zero there. A geodesic tangent to a boundary torus at a finite time does not enter the open neck at that time.",
        "source_id": "PM-358.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-elementary-passages",
        "exact_quote": "Let $V$ be the restriction of a smooth compactly supported field along $\\gamma$ to an entire neck passage $J$. If $J=\\mathbb R$, or $v$ never vanishes on $J$, or the passage is a turn contained in one end portion $0\\le\\tau\\le1/4$, then $$I_J^\\perp(V)\\ge0.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-358.1"
      },

          "source_id": "PM-358.1"
        }
      ],

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-turning-gauge",
        "exact_quote": "Consider a turning passage with data <a href=\"#eq:turn-data\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:turn-data\">[eq:turn-data]</a>– <a href=\"#eq:turn-incidence\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:turn-incidence\">[eq:turn-incidence]</a>. For every smooth field $V$ on $[0,L]$, there is a tangentially equivalent smooth field, denoted again by $V=(w,z)$, such that $$w(0)=w(L)=0,\\qquad \\langle V,T\\rangle\\ \\text{is affine in }s.$$ Put $\\Delta z=z(L)-z(0)$, $e=(1,0)^{\\mathsf T}$, and $\\mu=\\kappa v_0/4>0$. For this field, $$\\begin{aligned}\n I_{[0,L]}^\\perp(V)\n &=E_{[0,L]}(V)+\\frac{\\kappa v_0}{2}\n       \\bigl(z_1(0)^2+z_1(L)^2\\bigr)\n       -\\frac{(p^{\\mathsf T}\\Delta z)^2}{L}\n       \\label{eq:turning-index-identity}\\\\\n &\\ge E_{[0,L]}(V)+\\mu(e^{\\mathsf T}\\Delta z)^2\n       -\\frac{(p^{\\mathsf T}\\Delta z)^2}{L}.\n       \\label{eq:turning-index-reduction}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The same left side is the normal index of the original field.",
        "source_id": "PM-358.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-turning-gauge",
          "exact_quote": "Consider a turning passage with data <a href=\"#eq:turn-data\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:turn-data\">[eq:turn-data]</a>– <a href=\"#eq:turn-incidence\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:turn-incidence\">[eq:turn-incidence]</a>. For every smooth field $V$ on $[0,L]$, there is a tangentially equivalent smooth field, denoted again by $V=(w,z)$, such that $$w(0)=w(L)=0,\\qquad \\langle V,T\\rangle\\ \\text{is affine in }s.$$ Put $\\Delta z=z(L)-z(0)$, $e=(1,0)^{\\mathsf T}$, and $\\mu=\\kappa v_0/4>0$. For this field, $$\\begin{aligned}\n I_{[0,L]}^\\perp(V)\n &=E_{[0,L]}(V)+\\frac{\\kappa v_0}{2}\n       \\bigl(z_1(0)^2+z_1(L)^2\\bigr)\n       -\\frac{(p^{\\mathsf T}\\Delta z)^2}{L}\n       \\label{eq:turning-index-identity}\\\\\n &\\ge E_{[0,L]}(V)+\\mu(e^{\\mathsf T}\\Delta z)^2\n       -\\frac{(p^{\\mathsf T}\\Delta z)^2}{L}.\n       \\label{eq:turning-index-reduction}\n\\end{aligned}$$ The same left side is the normal index of the original field.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **No conjugate points** ([source](#pm-h-2)): A Riemannian metric has *no conjugate points* if no nonzero Jacobi field along a geodesic vanishes at two distinct times.
- **No focal points** ([source](#pm-h-2)): Recall that a metric has *no focal points* if, for every nonzero normal Jacobi field $J$ along a unit-speed geodesic with $J(0)=0$, one has $\frac{d}{ds}\|J(s)\|^2>0$ for $s>0$.
- **Cometric** ([source](#eq:cometric-path)): For a positive definite matrix $P$, its inverse $Q=P^{-1}$ is the matrix of the torus metric; we therefore call $P$ the cometric.
- **Semidefinite order** ([source](#pm-h-7)): For symmetric matrices $R,S$, write $R\preceq S$ when $S-R$ is positive semidefinite; $R\succeq S$ has the reversed meaning.
- **Neck passage** ([source](#pm-h-12)): A *neck passage* is a connected component $J$ of $\{s\in\mathbb R:0<t(\gamma(s))<1\}$.
- **Intrinsic and perpendicular index forms** ([source](#eq:normal-index-definition)): Let $\gamma$ be a complete unit-speed geodesic, and write $T=\dot\gamma$. For a smooth field $V$ along $\gamma$ and an interval $J$, define $$\begin{aligned} I_J(V)&=\int_J\bigl(\|D_sV\|^2- \langle R(V,T)T,V\rangle\bigr)\,ds,\label{eq:index-definition}\\ q_V&=\langle V,T\rangle,\qquad I_J^\perp(V)=I_J(V)-\int_J\|\dot q_V\|^2\,ds. \label{eq:normal-index-definition} \end{aligned}$$
- **Coordinate energy** ([source](#pm-h-11)): On a compact coordinate interval $[a,c]$, define $$\label{eq:linear-energy} E_{[a,c]}(V)=\int_a^c \left(\dot w^2+\|\dot z-P'p\,w\|_Q^2 -\tfrac12F''(t)w^2\right)\,ds.$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-thm-no-cat0-action) / [proof](#proof-stmt-thm-no-cat0-action): Flat torus and cyclic minset splittings turn boundary commutators into incompatible orthogonality conditions. -> No proper cocompact action on a nonempty proper nonpositively curved comparison space.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-prop-no-npc) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-no-npc): Lift a hypothetical locally nonpositively curved length metric and apply the action obstruction. -> No smooth nonpositively curved metric on the constructed manifold.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-lem-cometric-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-cometric-bounds): Control a small moving-frame perturbation of a diagonal concave cometric. -> Uniform positivity, concavity and derivative domination.
- **R3a** [claim](#eq:collar-warp) / [proof](#pm-h-9): Attach convex warped collars to truncated hyperbolic cusps using exact germ agreement and choose periods after the construction parameter. -> A smooth closed metric with nonpositive curvature outside the open neck.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-lem-neck-passages) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-neck-passages): Use concavity and smooth scalar geodesic uniqueness to classify every entire visit. -> Constant-height, monotone and single-turn passage alternatives.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-lem-turning-gauge) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-turning-gauge): Apply tangential invariance and endpoint correction to normalize the field. -> Coordinate energy plus a positive boundary penalty minus the tangential contribution.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-prop-turning-index) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-turning-s-bounds-2): Combine the timing margin, duality and quadratic minimization with a shared small parameter choice. -> Nonnegative perpendicular index on every returning passage.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-prop-passage-index) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-passage-index): Combine elementary visits and turning estimates without changing the original intrinsic integrals. -> Nonnegative intrinsic index on every entire visit.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-prop-global-assembly) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-global-assembly): Use absolute integrability and countable additivity, including the measurable complement. -> Global nonnegativity of the original index form.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-lem-index-criterion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-index-criterion): Extend nonnegativity to compactly supported Sobolev fields and test the derivative jump of a truncated Jacobi field. -> Absence of conjugate points.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-index-criterion-2): Combine the metric construction and global index criterion with the independent topological obstruction. -> The claimed manifold-level counterexample.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-lattice**: The endpoint period vectors give bases of the same lattice, satisfy the specified gluing, and reverse boundary orientation.
- **I-metric**: The constructed manifold carries a smooth metric without conjugate points and carries no smooth metric of nonpositive sectional curvature.
- **I-endpoints**: A single sufficiently small construction parameter gives nonnegative perpendicular index form for every returning passage with arbitrary endpoint values.
- **I-action**: The fundamental group of the constructed manifold has no proper cocompact isometric action on a nonempty proper CAT(0) space.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-curvature**: Infer existence of a nonpositively curved metric from absence of conjugate points. -> The constructed manifold is a counterexample to that implication.
- **A-boundary**: Certify neck passages by discarding the endpoint correction in the coordinate energy. -> Coordinate second variation differs from the intrinsic index form by an endpoint term.
- **A-scope**: Apply the CAT(0) action obstruction to arbitrary graph manifold groups. -> The stated obstruction is for the group arising from the specified gluing.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The torus-period realization is a finite constructive lattice calculation. This panel checks that realization only; the smooth metric construction and the index-form estimates concern all complete geodesics and are outside this finite check.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON containing positive rational strings epsilon and B2.
# PRECONDITIONS: Both rationals are strictly positive; B1=epsilon*B2.
# OUTPUTS: Exact rational endpoint period vectors and integral gluing determinant.
# TIME: Constant number of exact rational operations; bit cost depends on input length.
# SPACE: Constant number of rationals; bit storage depends on input length.
# ARITHMETIC: Certified exact python-flint fmpq arithmetic, no floating point.
# FAILURE: Invalid JSON or nonpositive rationals raise an exception.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpq
data = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
eps = fmpq(data['epsilon'])
period = fmpq(data['B2'])
if eps <= 0 or period <= 0:
    raise ValueError('positive rationals required')
B1 = eps * period
basis = ((B1, fmpq(0)), (fmpq(0), period))
change = ((1, 1), (1, 0))
target = [[sum(basis[k][r] * change[k][c] for k in range(2)) for r in range(2)] for c in range(2)]
det = change[0][0] * change[1][1] - change[0][1] * change[1][0]
result = {'h1': [str(x) for x in basis[0]], 'f1': [str(x) for x in basis[1]], 'h2': [str(x) for x in target[0]], 'f2': [str(x) for x in target[1]], 'det': det}
print(json.dumps(result, sort_keys=True))
```

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (counterexample_scope): The existence counterexample separates a metric without conjugate points from the existence of a nonpositively curved metric on its underlying manifold. Blocked use: ['Infer a nonpositively curved metric from absence of conjugate points.']
- **L2** (counterexample_scope): The action obstruction concerns the fundamental group of the specified torus-glued manifold and requires a proper cocompact isometric action on a nonempty proper CAT(0) space. Blocked use: ['Generalize the obstruction to arbitrary groups, or drop properness or cocompactness.']
- **L3** (counterexample_scope): The length-metric obstruction requires local CAT(0) geometry and compatibility with the manifold topology. Blocked use: ['Exclude every length metric or every unrelated metric-space representation.']
- **L4** (hypothesis): The small construction parameter must satisfy the cometric estimates and the returning-passage restriction before the boundary periods are chosen. Blocked use: ['Treat an arbitrary positive construction parameter or arbitrary boundary periods as certified.']
- **L5** (nonconstructive): The smooth cutoff is fixed subject to support and monotonicity conditions; the derivative constants depend on that choice. An explicit certified numerical parameter for an unspecified cutoff is ABSENT. Blocked use: ['Advertise an effective numerical parameter or a finite running time for the full metric construction.']
- **L6** (edge_case): Returning-passage divisions require positive duration and incidence, a nonzero boundary momentum component, and a positive boundary penalty. Constant-height and monotone passages are handled separately. Blocked use: ['Evaluate returning-passage divisions on a constant-height or grazing trajectory.']
- **L7** (edge_case): The passage estimate allows arbitrary endpoint values; it does not assert that passagewise gauges fit together. Blocked use: ['Assemble auxiliary gauge fields into a global smooth field without an additional argument.']
- **L8** (hypothesis): Global assembly requires completeness, nonpositive sectional curvature outside the open region, and passage estimates for every complete unit-speed geodesic and smooth compactly supported field. Blocked use: ['Replace the universal passage hypothesis by finitely many sampled trajectories.']
- **L9** (edge_case): Global assembly permits infinitely many passages and accumulating endpoints, including time on the interfaces. Blocked use: ['Truncate the passage decomposition or discard interface contributions without justification.']
- **L10** (approximation): Exact finite algebraic identities in the gluing or cusp model do not certify the smooth metric estimates or absence of conjugate points along every complete geodesic. Blocked use: ['Publish finite algebraic or numerical samples as a certificate of the entire existence theorem.']
- **L11** (counterexample_scope): The constructed manifold also admits no metric without focal points; absence of conjugate points cannot be strengthened to that condition. Blocked use: ['Infer absence of focal points from absence of conjugate points.']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 358 (`lean/docs/358.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [A Three-Manifold Without Conjugate Points and Without a Nonpositively Curved Metric](../../preprints/A-Three-Manifold-Without-Conjugate-Points-and-Without-a-Nonpositively-Curved-Metric-September-24-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalized result separates absence of conjugate points from nonpositive sectional curvature. It constructs a closed connected orientable smooth three-manifold with a smooth Riemannian metric having no conjugate points, while the same manifold admits no smooth metric of everywhere nonpositive sectional curvature. The no-conjugate-points assertion ranges over all geodesics and tangent-bundle Jacobi fields, including constant geodesics and unbounded time intervals. The separate no-focal-points and CAT(0) consequences are not included.

Comparator source `ConjugatePoints.lean` (4476 bytes, sha256 `e8b4c4bb21f1dc3fa18a2d21fc9ca923b54c012e07174e854e3b338bd89f9b16`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section
open Set Manifold Bundle
open scoped ContDiff

namespace ThreeManifold

abbrev E3 := Fin 3 → ℝ
abbrev I3 := 𝓘(ℝ, E3)

variable {M : Type} [TopologicalSpace M] [ChartedSpace E3 M]

def OrientedAtlas : Prop :=
  ∀ (c d : OpenPartialHomeomorph M E3), c ∈ atlas E3 M → d ∈ atlas E3 M →
    ∀ x ∈ c.target, c.symm x ∈ d.source →
      0 < LinearMap.det (fderiv ℝ (d ∘ c.symm) x).toLinearMap

variable [IsManifold I3 ∞ M]

abbrev SmoothMetric (M : Type) [TopologicalSpace M] [ChartedSpace E3 M]
    [IsManifold I3 ∞ M] :=
  ContMDiffRiemannianMetric I3 ∞ E3 (fun x : M => TangentSpace I3 x)

def basisVector (i : Fin 3) : E3 := Pi.single i 1

def metricMatrix (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : OpenPartialHomeomorph M E3) (x : E3) :
    Matrix (Fin 3) (Fin 3) ℝ :=
  fun i j => g.inner (c.symm x)
    (mfderiv I3 I3 c.symm x (basisVector i))
    (mfderiv I3 I3 c.symm x (basisVector j))

def coordPartial (f : E3 → ℝ) (x : E3) (i : Fin 3) : ℝ :=
  fderiv ℝ f x (basisVector i)

def christoffel (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : OpenPartialHomeomorph M E3)
    (x : E3) (i j k : Fin 3) : ℝ :=
  (1 / 2 : ℝ) * ∑ l : Fin 3, (metricMatrix g c x)⁻¹ i l *
    (coordPartial (fun y => metricMatrix g c y l k) x j +
     coordPartial (fun y => metricMatrix g c y l j) x k -
     coordPartial (fun y => metricMatrix g c y j k) x l)

def connectionTerm (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : OpenPartialHomeomorph M E3)
    (x u v : E3) : E3 :=
  fun i => ∑ j : Fin 3, ∑ k : Fin 3, christoffel g c x i j k * u j * v k

def curvatureCoefficient (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : OpenPartialHomeomorph M E3)
    (x : E3) (l k i j : Fin 3) : ℝ :=
  coordPartial (fun y => christoffel g c y l j k) x i -
  coordPartial (fun y => christoffel g c y l i k) x j +
    ∑ a : Fin 3, (christoffel g c x l i a * christoffel g c x a j k -
      christoffel g c x l j a * christoffel g c x a i k)

def curvatureTerm (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : OpenPartialHomeomorph M E3)
    (x u v w : E3) : E3 :=
  fun l => ∑ k : Fin 3, ∑ i : Fin 3, ∑ j : Fin 3,
    curvatureCoefficient g c x l k i j * w k * u i * v j

def NonpositiveSectionalCurvature (g : SmoothMetric M) : Prop :=
  ∀ (c : OpenPartialHomeomorph M E3), c ∈ atlas E3 M →
    ∀ x ∈ c.target, ∀ u v : E3,
      (∑ i : Fin 3, ∑ j : Fin 3,
        metricMatrix g c x i j * curvatureTerm g c x u v v i * u j) ≤ 0

def alongDerivative (g : SmoothMetric M) (c : OpenPartialHomeomorph M E3)
    (x w : ℝ → E3) (s : ℝ) : E3 :=
  deriv w s + connectionTerm g c (x s) (deriv x s) (w s)

def IsGeodesicOn (g : SmoothMetric M) (γ : ℝ → M) (U : Set ℝ) : Prop :=
  ContMDiffOn 𝓘(ℝ, ℝ) I3 ∞ γ U ∧
    ∀ (c : OpenPartialHomeomorph M E3), c ∈ atlas E3 M →
      ∀ s ∈ U, γ s ∈ c.source →
        alongDerivative g c (c ∘ γ) (deriv (c ∘ γ)) s = 0

def fieldCoordinates (γ : ℝ → M) (J : ∀ s, TangentSpace I3 (γ s))
    (c : OpenPartialHomeomorph M E3) (s : ℝ) : E3 :=
  mfderiv I3 I3 c (γ s) (J s)

def IsJacobiFieldOn (g : SmoothMetric M) (γ : ℝ → M)
    (J : ∀ s, TangentSpace I3 (γ s)) (U : Set ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∀ (c : OpenPartialHomeomorph M E3), c ∈ atlas E3 M →
    ContDiffOn ℝ ∞ (fieldCoordinates γ J c) (U ∩ γ ⁻¹' c.source) ∧
    ∀ s ∈ U, γ s ∈ c.source →
      alongDerivative g c (c ∘ γ)
        (alongDerivative g c (c ∘ γ) (fieldCoordinates γ J c)) s +
      curvatureTerm g c (c (γ s)) (fieldCoordinates γ J c s)
        (deriv (c ∘ γ) s) (deriv (c ∘ γ) s) = 0

def NoConjugatePoints (g : SmoothMetric M) : Prop :=
  ∀ (U : Set ℝ), IsOpen U → OrdConnected U →
    ∀ (γ : ℝ → M), IsGeodesicOn g γ U →
      ∀ (J : ∀ s, TangentSpace I3 (γ s)), IsJacobiFieldOn g γ J U →
        ∀ a ∈ U, ∀ b ∈ U, a ≠ b → J a = 0 → J b = 0 → ∀ s ∈ U, J s = 0

def MainStatement : Prop :=
  ∃ (M : Type) (top : TopologicalSpace M),
    letI := top
    ∃ (_ : T2Space M) (_ : SecondCountableTopology M) (_ : CompactSpace M)
      (_ : ConnectedSpace M) (charts : ChartedSpace E3 M),
      letI := charts
      ∃ (smooth : IsManifold I3 ∞ M),
        letI := smooth
        OrientedAtlas (M := M) ∧
          (∃ g : SmoothMetric M, NoConjugatePoints g) ∧
          ¬ ∃ g : SmoothMetric M, NonpositiveSectionalCurvature g

theorem main_theorem : MainStatement := by
  sorry

end ThreeManifold

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We construct a closed connected orientable smooth three-manifold that admits a smooth Riemannian metric without conjugate points but admits no smooth Riemannian metric of nonpositive sectional curvature.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

A Riemannian metric has *no conjugate points* if no nonzero Jacobi field along a geodesic vanishes at two distinct times. Nonpositive sectional curvature implies this condition. Gulliver showed that the converse fails for an individual metric: local modifications of negatively curved metrics can produce regions of positive curvature while preserving the absence of conjugate points (Gulliver 1975, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Gulliver1975](#ref-Gulliver1975)]. Those examples retain a manifold that already admits a negatively curved metric. The existence question is therefore different: must a closed manifold carrying a metric without conjugate points also carry some metric of nonpositive sectional curvature?

This question appears in Ivanov and Kapovitch (Ivanov and Kapovitch 2014, Questions 1.1 and 8.1) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[IvanovKapovitch2014](#ref-IvanovKapovitch2014)] and in the survey of Burns and Matveev (Burns and Matveev 2021, Question 4.1.2) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[BurnsMatveev2021](#ref-BurnsMatveev2021)], which explicitly includes dimension three. A closed surface without conjugate points has universal cover diffeomorphic to $\mathbb R^2$ by the exponential map, and hence has nonpositive Euler characteristic. Uniformization then supplies a flat or negatively curved metric, so the existence problem begins in dimension three. Ivanov and Kapovitch identify graph manifolds as a central three-dimensional test case and ask about gluings of two punctured-torus products (Ivanov and Kapovitch 2014, sec. 8) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[IvanovKapovitch2014](#ref-IvanovKapovitch2014)]. We give a negative answer in dimension three, using a metric on a two-piece graph manifold whose topology obstructs nonpositive curvature.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There exists a closed connected orientable smooth three-manifold $M$ with a $C^\infty$ Riemannian metric without conjugate points, such that $M$ admits no $C^\infty$ Riemannian metric whose sectional curvature is everywhere at most zero.*

</div>

The obstruction is stronger than the Riemannian assertion in the theorem. The group $\pi_1(M)$ admits no proper cocompact isometric action on a nonempty proper $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space (Theorem <a href="#thm:no-cat0-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:no-cat0-action">2</a>); consequently, $M$ admits no locally $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ length metric inducing its topology (Corollary <a href="#cor:no-local-cat0" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:no-local-cat0">3</a>).

The same manifold also admits no metric without focal points. Recall that a metric has *no focal points* if, for every nonzero normal Jacobi field $J$ along a unit-speed geodesic with $J(0)=0$, one has $\frac{d}{ds}|J(s)|^2>0$ for $s>0$. This condition is stronger than absence of conjugate points. Ivanov and Kapovitch prove that a closed three-manifold admits a metric without focal points if and only if it admits a metric of nonpositive sectional curvature (Ivanov and Kapovitch 2014, Theorem 7.1) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[IvanovKapovitch2014](#ref-IvanovKapovitch2014)]. Their equivalence and Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> give the stated additional obstruction.

The manifold is obtained by gluing two copies of $\Sigma\times S^1$, where $\Sigma$ is a compact oriented surface of genus one with one boundary component. Write $h_i$ for the surface boundary and $f_i$ for the circle factor in the $i$th boundary torus. The gluing is $$h_2=h_1+f_1,\qquad f_2=h_1.$$ This is an instance of Leeb’s two-piece graph-manifold obstruction (Leeb 1995, sec. 4.1, Example 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Leeb1995](#ref-Leeb1995)]. We include the group-theoretic proof, which also gives the stronger action obstruction above. Under a hypothetical nonpositively curved metric, the common torus group acts by translations on a Euclidean plane. In each product piece, the surface boundary is a commutator of elements commuting with the fiber. The resulting translation vectors must be perpendicular. These two orthogonality conditions are incompatible with the gluing.

The main work is the metric construction and its geodesic estimate. Each product piece has nonpositive sectional curvature. Between them we insert a neck $[0,1]\times T^2$ with metric $$g=dt^2+dx^{\mathsf T}P(t)^{-1}\,dx,$$ where $P(t)$ is a smooth path of positive definite matrices. We call $P$ the *cometric*, since it is the inverse torus metric. We choose it concave, meaning that $-P''$ is positive semidefinite, with exact product formulas near the ends. A small parameter $\delta$ controls its derivatives; a shear of size $\delta^2$ realizes the integral gluing after the torus periods are chosen. Convex warped collars then join the endpoints smoothly to punctured-torus cores.

For the absence of conjugate points, we use the classical index-form framework (Milnor 1963, vol. 51, secs. 13–15) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[Milnor1963](#ref-Milnor1963)]. Concavity of $P$ makes the coordinate second variation nonnegative, but the intrinsic index form differs by endpoint terms. The estimates must allow arbitrary field values at the ends of an entire neck passage, so that they can be combined with the estimates in the product pieces. A tangential change of field handles passages with no turn. A returning geodesic requires an estimate over its whole excursion.

For such an excursion, a positive boundary term controls one component of the endpoint change in the torus part of the variation field. We combine this control with the coordinate energy by Hilbert-space Cauchy–Schwarz and a quadratic minimization. A strict timing margin for excursions leaving an endpoint collar makes the estimate uniform in the entry angle, turning height, and excursion time. No upper bound on the duration is needed. This is what permits one metric to handle every complete geodesic.

Section <a href="#sec:topology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:topology">2</a> fixes the manifold and proves its curvature obstruction. Section <a href="#sec:cometric" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cometric">3</a> constructs the metric and establishes the matrix estimates. Section <a href="#sec:variations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:variations">4</a> derives the index identities and classifies neck passages, and Section <a href="#sec:turning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:turning">5</a> proves the returning-passage estimate. Section <a href="#sec:global" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:global">6</a> sums the intrinsic index integrals of the original field over all visits, including infinitely many visits, and completes the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Appendix <a href="#app:punctured-torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:punctured-torus">7</a> records the hyperbolic cusp model used for the compact product cores.

<a id="sec:topology"></a>
### The manifold and the obstruction to nonpositive curvature

We first choose the underlying manifold. Its two product pieces impose incompatible orthogonality conditions on the gluing torus under any nonpositively curved metric. This is an instance of Leeb’s two-piece graph-manifold obstruction (Leeb 1995, sec. 4.1, Example 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Leeb1995](#ref-Leeb1995)]. The obstruction to proper cocompact actions on proper CAT(0) spaces is also contained in the group-theoretic framework of Kapovich and Leeb (Kapovich and Leeb 1996, Theorem 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[KapovichLeeb1996](#ref-KapovichLeeb1996)]. We give the group-theoretic argument explicitly, since it fixes the gluing convention used in the metric construction.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Two product pieces and one shear

<a id="eq:torus-gluing"></a>
Let $\Sigma$ be a compact connected oriented surface of genus one with one boundary component, and let $N_i=\Sigma\times S^1$ for $i=1,2$. On $\partial N_i$ write $h_i$ for the oriented boundary circle of $\Sigma$ and $f_i$ for the positively oriented circle factor. Thus $(h_i,f_i)$ is an oriented integral basis of $\pi_1(\partial N_i)\cong\mathbb Z^2$. Choose the linear diffeomorphism $\varphi:\partial N_2\longrightarrow
\partial N_1$ whose action on these bases is $$\label{eq:torus-gluing}
 h_2=h_1+f_1,\qquad f_2=h_1.$$ Here and below additive notation is used only inside the torus group. The matrix of $\varphi_*$ is $\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&1\\1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)$, with determinant $-1$, so $\varphi$ reverses the induced boundary orientations. Gluing the two pieces by $\varphi$ therefore gives a closed connected oriented smooth three-manifold $M$. More explicitly, collar coordinates give smooth charts across the identified boundary; the compact pieces cover $M$, every boundary component is paired, and the common torus makes the union connected. We insert a product cylinder $[0,1]\times T^2$ between the two pieces. Inserting this collar leaves the diffeomorphism type unchanged and provides the region where the metric will vary.

<a id="eq:torus-periods"></a>
The coordinate realization needed later is worth recording. For any $\epsilon>0$ and $B_2>0$, put $B_1=\epsilon B_2$ and $$T^2=\mathbb R^2/\Lambda,\qquad
 \Lambda=\mathbb Z B_1e_1+\mathbb Z B_2e_2,$$ where $e_1,e_2$ are the standard coordinate vectors. At the first end use the frame $(e_1,e_2)$ with respective coordinate periods $(B_1,B_2)$. At the second end use the frame $(e_2+\epsilon e_1,e_1)$ with respective periods $(B_2,B_1)$. The first vector in each frame is the surface-boundary direction and the second is the fiber direction. Their period vectors are $$\label{eq:torus-periods}
 \begin{aligned}
 h_1&: B_1e_1,& f_1&:B_2e_2,\\
 h_2&:B_2(e_2+\epsilon e_1)=B_1e_1+B_2e_2,
 &f_2&:B_1e_1.
 \end{aligned}$$ In particular the second pair is a basis of exactly the same lattice, and these physical periods implement <a href="#eq:torus-gluing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:torus-gluing">[eq:torus-gluing]</a>.

<a id="eq:boundary-commutators"></a>
Choose basepoints and connecting paths through the cylinder so that the boundary groups are identified by <a href="#eq:torus-gluing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:torus-gluing">[eq:torus-gluing]</a>. For suitable free generators $a_i,b_i$ of $\pi_1(\Sigma)$, the boundary element is $h_i=[a_i,b_i]^{\pm1}$. This cyclically reduced nonempty word has infinite order in the free group. Consequently $$G_i:=\pi_1(N_i)=F(a_i,b_i)\times\langle f_i\rangle,
 \qquad
 \langle h_i,f_i\rangle\cong\mathbb Z^2$$ and the boundary-group inclusion in each $G_i$ is injective: a relation $h_i^m f_i^n=1$ first gives $n=0$ by projection onto the circle factor and then $m=0$ in the free group. Van Kampen’s theorem and the normal form for an amalgamated product give <a id="eq:amalgam"></a>$$\label{eq:amalgam}
 \Gamma:=\pi_1(M)=G_1*_{\Gamma_T}G_2,
 \qquad \Gamma_T=\langle h_1,f_1\rangle=\langle h_2,f_2\rangle\cong\mathbb Z^2,$$ with both vertex groups and $\Gamma_T$ embedded in $\Gamma$. For $f\in\Gamma$, write $C_\Gamma(f)=\{g\in\Gamma:gf=fg\}$ for its centralizer. Since $f_i$ is central in $G_i$, we have $$\label{eq:boundary-commutators}
 G_i\subset C_\Gamma(f_i),\qquad
 h_i\in[C_\Gamma(f_i),C_\Gamma(f_i)]\quad(i=1,2).$$

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### The two incompatible orthogonality conditions

<a id="stmt-thm-no-cat0-action"></a>
<div id="thm:no-cat0-action" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2**. *The fundamental group $\Gamma=\pi_1(M)$ of the closed three-manifold defined by <a href="#eq:torus-gluing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:torus-gluing">[eq:torus-gluing]</a> admits no proper cocompact isometric action on a nonempty proper $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-no-cat0-action"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-no-cat0-action</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose that $\Gamma$ acts properly and cocompactly by isometries on a nonempty proper $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space $X$. Properness of the action means that for compact sets $K,L\subset X$, only finitely many $g\in\Gamma$ satisfy $gK\cap L\ne\varnothing$; in particular, point stabilizers are finite.

For $g\in\Gamma$ set $$\ell(g)=\inf_{x\in X}d(x,gx),\qquad
 \operatorname{Min}(g)=\{x\in X:d(x,gx)=\ell(g)\}.$$ We check the attainment of this infimum before applying the flat torus theorem. Choose $x_n$ with $d(x_n,gx_n)\to\ell(g)$ and a compact set $K\subset X$ whose $\Gamma$-translates cover $X$. There are $k_n\in\Gamma$ such that $y_n=k_nx_n\in K$. Put $g_n=k_ngk_n^{-1}$. For all large $n$, $d(y_n,g_ny_n)\le\ell(g)+1$. The closed $(\ell(g)+1)$-neighborhood of $K$ is compact, because $X$ is proper. Properness of the action therefore permits only finitely many $g_n$. After passage to a subsequence, $g_n=g_*$ and $y_n\to y\in K$, whence $d(y,g_*y)=\ell(g)$. Since translation length is invariant under conjugation, conjugating $y$ back gives a point of $\operatorname{Min}(g)$. Thus every element of $\Gamma$ is semisimple. If $g$ has infinite order, its attained minimum is positive: otherwise $g$ fixes a point, and its infinitely many distinct powers belong to that point stabilizer, contrary to its finiteness. The semisimplicity argument is also given in (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Proposition II.6.10(2)) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BridsonHaefliger1999](#ref-BridsonHaefliger1999)].

Apply the flat torus theorem (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Theorem II.7.1(1)–(3)) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BridsonHaefliger1999](#ref-BridsonHaefliger1999)] to the embedded group $\Gamma_T\cong\mathbb Z^2$. Its action is proper and semisimple by the preceding paragraph. Hence $$\operatorname{Min}(\Gamma_T):=\bigcap_{a\in\Gamma_T}\operatorname{Min}(a)
       \cong Y\times\mathbb R^2,$$ and $\Gamma_T$ acts trivially on $Y$ and as a translation lattice on $\mathbb R^2$. Fix one of these invariant Euclidean planes, denoted $E$, and write $\mathbf v(a)\in\mathbb R^2$ for the translation vector of $a\in\Gamma_T$. The map $\mathbf v$ is a homomorphism, and its values on any integral basis of $\Gamma_T$ are linearly independent.

<a id="eq:cyclic-minset"></a>
We now use the boundary-commutator relation <a href="#eq:boundary-commutators" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:boundary-commutators">[eq:boundary-commutators]</a>. For each $i$, the positive translation length $\ell_i=\ell(f_i)$ and the cyclic minset theorem give $$\label{eq:cyclic-minset}
 \operatorname{Min}(f_i)\cong Y_i\times\mathbb R,
 \qquad f_i(y,t)=(y,t+\ell_i).$$ Every $g\in C_\Gamma(f_i)$ preserves this product and acts as $$g(y,t)=(g_i'(y),t+\tau_i(g)),$$ where $\tau_i(g)$ is a real number independent of $y$; see (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Theorem II.6.8(4)–(5)) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[BridsonHaefliger1999](#ref-BridsonHaefliger1999)]. To see the reason for this product action, commutation sends each oriented $f_i$-axis to another such axis. In the splitting <a href="#eq:cyclic-minset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cyclic-minset">[eq:cyclic-minset]</a>, these are the lines $\{y\}\times\mathbb R$. An axiswise translation could initially have a shift depending on $y$, but preservation of the product distance between $(y,t)$ and $(z,s)$ for every $t-s\in\mathbb R$ forces the two shifts to agree. Composition now shows that $\tau_i:C_\Gamma(f_i)\longrightarrow(\mathbb R,+)$ is a homomorphism. It vanishes on commutators, so $\tau_i(h_i)=0$.

<a id="eq:torus-orthogonality"></a>
The same plane $E$ lies in both cyclic minsets, because $E\subset\operatorname{Min}(\Gamma_T)$. At a point $x=(y,t)\in E$ expressed in the $i$th splitting, the zero shift of $h_i$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
 d(x,h_if_ix)^2
 &=d_{Y_i}(y,h_i'y)^2+\ell_i^2\\
 &=d(x,h_ix)^2+d(x,f_ix)^2.
 \end{aligned}$$ All three distances can also be computed in the Euclidean plane $E$. It follows that $$\label{eq:torus-orthogonality}
 \langle\mathbf v(h_i),\mathbf v(f_i)\rangle=0
 \qquad(i=1,2).$$ Put $u=\mathbf v(h_1)$ and $v=\mathbf v(f_1)$. The first equality in <a href="#eq:torus-orthogonality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:torus-orthogonality">[eq:torus-orthogonality]</a> says $\langle u,v\rangle=0$. The gluing relations give $\mathbf v(h_2)=u+v$ and $\mathbf v(f_2)=u$, so the second equality says $$0=\langle u+v,u\rangle=|u|^2.$$ This is impossible: $h_1$ is nontrivial in $\Gamma_T$, and its translation vector in the lattice is nonzero. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-cor-no-local-cat0"></a>
<div id="cor:no-local-cat0" class="corollary">

**Corollary 3**. *The manifold $M$ admits no locally $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ length metric inducing its manifold topology.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-no-local-cat0"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-no-local-cat0</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose that such a metric exists. Compactness of $M$ makes it complete and locally compact. Its ordinary universal cover, equipped with the lifted length metric, is complete and locally compact, and the covering map is a local isometry. Metric Cartan–Hadamard makes this universal cover a $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ space, and metric Hopf–Rinow makes it proper; see (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Proposition I.3.7(1) and Theorem II.4.1(2)) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BridsonHaefliger1999](#ref-BridsonHaefliger1999)]. The deck group $\Gamma$ acts freely and properly by isometries, with compact quotient $M$. This contradicts Theorem <a href="#thm:no-cat0-action" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:no-cat0-action">2</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-no-npc"></a>
<div id="prop:no-npc" class="proposition">

**Proposition 4**. *The closed smooth three-manifold $M$ defined by <a href="#eq:torus-gluing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:torus-gluing">[eq:torus-gluing]</a> admits no smooth Riemannian metric of nonpositive sectional curvature.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-no-npc"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-no-npc</summary>

*Proof.* Such a Riemannian metric is a length metric inducing the manifold topology and is locally $\operatorname{CAT}(0)$ (Bridson and Haefliger 1999, Theorem II.1A.6) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[BridsonHaefliger1999](#ref-BridsonHaefliger1999)], contradicting Corollary <a href="#cor:no-local-cat0" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:no-local-cat0">3</a>. ◻

</details>

These obstructions depend only on the topology of $M$. The metric constructed below will have no conjugate points.

<a id="sec:cometric"></a>
### A concave cometric and smooth product cores

We construct the metric in two stages. First we join the two product structures by a metric on the torus neck. Then we extend its endpoint formulas over compact punctured-torus products. The neck estimates will depend on a small parameter $\delta$, while the torus periods will be chosen afterward to fit the surface boundaries into hyperbolic cusps.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The matrix path and its endpoint coordinates

Write $e_1,e_2$ for the standard basis of $\mathbb R^2$. The two product structures require different diagonal coordinate frames: $(e_1,e_2)$ on the left and $(e_2+\epsilon e_1,e_1)$ on the right. To arrange concavity, we start with a diagonal path and introduce the shear only where both diagonal coefficients have strictly negative second derivative. The diagonal variation has size $\delta$; taking $\epsilon=\delta^2$ makes the frame change a smaller perturbation. The periods $B_1=\epsilon B_2$ then turn this small coordinate shear into the fixed integral gluing of Section <a href="#sec:topology" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:topology">2</a>.

<a id="eq:cometric-path"></a>
Define the smooth function $a:\mathbb R\to[0,1)$ and its primitive $A$ by <a id="eq:cometric-cutoff"></a>$$\label{eq:cometric-cutoff}
 a(u)=\begin{cases}
       \exp\bigl(-1/(2/3-u)\bigr),&u<2/3,\\
       0,&u\ge 2/3,
      \end{cases}
 \qquad A(u)=\int_0^u a(y)\,dy.$$ The definition includes negative $u$, which will be needed when extending the metric into the product pieces. Fix once and for all a smooth nondecreasing function $\chi:\mathbb R\to[0,1]$ equal to zero on $(-\infty,5/12]$ and to one on $[7/12,\infty)$. For $0<\delta\le 1/2$, set $\epsilon=\delta^2$ and define $$\begin{aligned}
 H(t)&=e_2+\epsilon\chi(t)e_1,\notag\\
 d_1(t)&=1+\delta A(t),\qquad
 d_2(t)=1+\delta A(1-t),\notag\\
 P(t)&=d_1(t)e_1e_1^{\mathsf T}+d_2(t)H(t)H(t)^{\mathsf T},
 \qquad 0\le t\le1.
 \label{eq:cometric-path}
\end{aligned}$$ Here a superscript $\mathsf T$ denotes transpose. For a positive definite matrix $P$, its inverse $Q=P^{-1}$ is the matrix of the torus metric; we therefore call $P$ the cometric.

<a id="eq:end-frames"></a>
Choose $B_2>0$ later and put $B_1=\epsilon B_2$. On $$T^2=\mathbb R^2/\Lambda,\qquad
 \Lambda=\mathbb Z B_1e_1+\mathbb Z B_2e_2,$$ use the neck metric <a id="eq:neck-metric"></a>$$\label{eq:neck-metric}
 g=dt^2+dx^{\mathsf T}Q(t)\,dx.$$ The product coordinates at the two ends are given by the frames $$\label{eq:end-frames}
 T_1=(e_1,e_2),\qquad T_2=(e_2+\epsilon e_1,e_1),\qquad x=T_i y.$$ At the first end the two coordinate periods are $B_1,B_2$; at the second they are $B_2,B_1$. The first coordinate is the surface-boundary direction and the second is the circle factor. The period calculation <a href="#eq:torus-periods" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:torus-periods">[eq:torus-periods]</a> shows that these frames give exactly the gluing <a href="#eq:torus-gluing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:torus-gluing">[eq:torus-gluing]</a>.

<a id="eq:end-cometric"></a>
Let $\tau=t$ at the first end and $\tau=1-t$ at the second, so that $\tau$ increases into the neck. A constant coordinate change $x=T_i y$ changes the cometric by $P\mapsto T_i^{-1}PT_i^{-\mathsf T}$. Since $A$ is constant on $[2/3,\infty)$, in these endpoint coordinates we have the exact identities $$\label{eq:end-cometric}
 P_i(\tau)=\begin{pmatrix}1+\delta A(\tau)&0\\0&b\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad b=1+\delta A(1),\qquad 0\le\tau\le1/4.$$ In particular <a id="eq:end-cometric-slope"></a>$$\label{eq:end-cometric-slope}
 P_i(0)e_1=e_1,
 \qquad P_i'(0)=\kappa e_1e_1^{\mathsf T},
 \qquad \kappa=\delta a(0)>0.$$ Primes on $P_i$ refer to $\tau$.

For symmetric matrices $R,S$, write $R\preceq S$ when $S-R$ is positive semidefinite; $R\succeq S$ has the reversed meaning.

<a id="stmt-lem-cometric-bounds"></a>
<div id="lem:cometric-bounds" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5**. *There are constants $\delta_0>0$ and $C<\infty$, depending only on the fixed function $\chi$, such that for $0<\delta\le\delta_0$ the path <a href="#eq:cometric-path" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cometric-path">[eq:cometric-path]</a> satisfies, in the original coordinates and in either endpoint coordinate system, <a id="eq:cometric-uniform"></a><a id="eq:cometric-domination"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 \frac19\operatorname{Id}\preceq P\preceq18\operatorname{Id},
 \qquad
 \frac1{18}\operatorname{Id}\preceq Q\preceq9\operatorname{Id},
 \qquad \|P'\|\le C\delta,
 \label{eq:cometric-uniform}\\
 -P''\succeq0,
 \qquad |P'p|^2\le C\delta\,p^{\mathsf T}(-P'')p
 \quad\text{for every }p\in\mathbb R^2.
 \label{eq:cometric-domination}
\end{gathered}$$ The constants do not depend on $B_1$ or $B_2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cometric-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cometric-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* All matrix norms are Euclidean operator norms. On $[0,1]$ we have $1\le d_i\le2$. The matrix $R=(e_1,H)$ and its inverse have squared norms at most $3$. As $P=R\operatorname{diag}(d_1,d_2)R^{\mathsf T}$, this gives $\operatorname{Id}/3\preceq P\preceq6\operatorname{Id}$ in the original coordinates. The same bounds on $\|T_i\|^2$ and $\|T_i^{-1}\|^2$ give the displayed weaker bounds in both endpoint frames.

For $u<2/3$, <a id="eq:cutoff-differential"></a>$$\label{eq:cutoff-differential}
 a'(u)=-\frac{a(u)}{(2/3-u)^2}.$$ Consequently, on $[0,1]$, $a^2\le-a'$ and $|a'|\le1$, with both sides zero on the plateau when appropriate. Put $I_0=[5/12,7/12]$. Outside $I_0$, the vector $H$ is constant. If $q=R^{\mathsf T}p$, then $$p^{\mathsf T}(-P'')p
 =\delta\bigl(-a'(t)q_1^2-a'(1-t)q_2^2\bigr),$$ and $$|P'p|^2
 \le3\delta^2\bigl(a(t)^2q_1^2+a(1-t)^2q_2^2\bigr)
 \le3\delta\,p^{\mathsf T}(-P'')p.$$ This proves concavity and the required domination there, including the directions in which $-P''$ vanishes.

On $I_0$ both $t$ and $1-t$ lie in $[5/12,7/12]$, and $$-a'(t),\ -a'(1-t)\ge m,\qquad m=144e^{-12}>0.$$ To control every error caused by the moving frame, compare $P$ with $P_0=\operatorname{diag}(d_1,d_2)$ and write $E=P-P_0$. The derivatives are $$\begin{aligned}
 P'={}&\delta a(t)e_1e_1^{\mathsf T}
       -\delta a(1-t)HH^{\mathsf T}
       +d_2\epsilon\chi'(e_1H^{\mathsf T}+He_1^{\mathsf T}),\\
 P''={}&\delta a'(t)e_1e_1^{\mathsf T}
       +\delta a'(1-t)HH^{\mathsf T}
       -2\delta a(1-t)\epsilon\chi'
              (e_1H^{\mathsf T}+He_1^{\mathsf T})\\
       &+d_2\epsilon\chi''(e_1H^{\mathsf T}+He_1^{\mathsf T})
       +2d_2\epsilon^2(\chi')^2e_1e_1^{\mathsf T}.
\end{aligned}$$ Using $|H|\le\sqrt2$ and $\|HH^{\mathsf T}-e_2e_2^{\mathsf T}\|\le3\epsilon$ gives $$\|E'\|\le C_1\epsilon,
 \qquad \|E''\|\le C_2\epsilon,$$ where $C_1,C_2$ depend only on the fixed function $\chi$. These estimates use only $\delta,\epsilon\le1$ and $d_2\le2$. Take $\delta_0\le\min\{1/2,m/(2C_2)\}$. Since $\epsilon=\delta^2$ and $-P_0''\succeq m\delta\operatorname{Id}$, we obtain $$-P''\succeq\tfrac12m\delta\operatorname{Id},
 \qquad \|P'\|\le(1+C_1)\delta
 \quad\text{on }I_0.$$ It follows that $$|P'p|^2\le\frac{2(1+C_1)^2}{m}\,
              \delta\,p^{\mathsf T}(-P'')p.$$ The displayed derivative formula also gives $\|P'\|\le C\delta$ outside $I_0$.

Finally, under $\widetilde P=T_i^{-1}PT_i^{-\mathsf T}$, with $q=T_i^{-\mathsf T}p$, the domination estimate transforms as $$|\widetilde P'p|^2
 \le\|T_i^{-1}\|^2|P'q|^2
 \le3C\delta\,p^{\mathsf T}(-\widetilde P'')p.$$ Reversing $t$ at the second end changes the sign of the first derivative and leaves the second derivative unchanged. This proves all assertions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Smooth convex collars

The endpoint metric in <a href="#eq:end-cometric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:end-cometric">[eq:end-cometric]</a> is the product of a flat circle and the surface metric $$d\tau^2+\ell_0(\tau)^2dy_1^2,
 \qquad \ell_0(\tau)=(1+\delta A(\tau))^{-1/2}.$$ Its Gaussian curvature is $-\ell_0''/\ell_0$. Direct differentiation gives <a id="eq:end-warp-convexity"></a>$$\label{eq:end-warp-convexity}
 \frac{\ell_0''}{\ell_0}
 =\frac{3\delta^2a^2}{4(1+\delta A)^2}
  -\frac{\delta a'}{2(1+\delta A)}\ge0.$$ Thus the neck already has nonpositive sectional curvature on $0\le t\le1/4$ and $3/4\le t\le1$. We next extend each endpoint surface metric inward to a hyperbolic cusp by an explicit convex warping function.

<a id="eq:collar-cusp-germ"></a>
For $-1\le\tau\le0$ we have $A(\tau)\ge-1$, so $1+\delta A(\tau)\ge1/2$. Define $$r(\tau)=\frac{\delta a(\tau)}{2(1+\delta A(\tau))}.$$ For $0<\delta\le1/2$, this is positive and at most $\delta\le1/2$; moreover $$r'(\tau)=\frac{\delta\bigl(a'(\tau)(1+\delta A(\tau))
                                  -\delta a(\tau)^2\bigr)}
                         {2(1+\delta A(\tau))^2}<0.$$ Fix a smooth nondecreasing function $\lambda:[-1,0]\to[0,1]$ equal to zero near $-1$ and one near $0$. Put <a id="eq:collar-warp"></a>$$\label{eq:collar-warp}
 k=1-\lambda+\lambda r,
 \qquad \ell(\tau)=\exp\left(-\int_0^\tau k(u)\,du\right).$$ Then $k>0$ and $k'=\lambda'(r-1)+\lambda r'\le0$. Hence $$\ell''=(k^2-k')\ell>0.$$ Near zero, $k=r$ and $\ell(0)=1$, which proves the exact equality $\ell=(1+\delta A)^{-1/2}$ there. Near $-1$, $k=1$, so $$\label{eq:collar-cusp-germ}
 \ell(\tau)=\ell(-1)e^{-(\tau+1)}.$$ Both matching assertions hold on open collars, not only to a finite order at their endpoints.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Punctured-torus cores and the final gluing

<a id="eq:hyperbolic-cusp"></a>
Fix a complete finite-area hyperbolic punctured torus. It has an embedded cusp of the form $$\label{eq:hyperbolic-cusp}
 dr^2+c^2e^{-2r}d\theta^2,
 \qquad r\ge0,\quad \theta\in\mathbb R/\mathbb Z,$$ for some $c>0$. In particular, its cusp contains a horocycle of every sufficiently small positive length. An explicit ideal-quadrilateral construction, following (Bonahon 2009, vol. 49, sec. 5.5) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Bonahon2009](#ref-Bonahon2009)], is recalled in Appendix <a href="#app:punctured-torus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:punctured-torus">7</a>.

Fix $\delta$ to meet both Lemma <a href="#lem:cometric-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cometric-bounds">5</a> and the additional upper bounds imposed by the index-form estimates below. Only after this choice, choose $B_2$ so small that both numbers $B_i\ell(-1)$, $i=1,2$, occur as lengths of horocycles in an embedded cusp <a href="#eq:hyperbolic-cusp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hyperbolic-cusp">[eq:hyperbolic-cusp]</a>, and set $B_1=\delta^2B_2$ as above. For each $i$, truncate a copy of the punctured torus at the horocycle of length $B_i\ell(-1)$, retain the compact side, and attach $[-1,0]\times(\mathbb R/B_i\mathbb Z)$ with metric $d\tau^2+\ell(\tau)^2dy_1^2$. To check smoothness at $\tau=-1$, if the truncation is at $r=r_i$, set $$r=r_i+\tau+1,\qquad \theta=y_1/B_i.$$ The cusp metric becomes $d\tau^2+\ell(-1)^2e^{-2(\tau+1)}dy_1^2$ on a neighborhood of the seam, which agrees exactly with <a href="#eq:collar-cusp-germ" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:collar-cusp-germ">[eq:collar-cusp-germ]</a>. The resulting compact surface $\Sigma_i$ has genus one and one boundary component, and has Gaussian curvature at most zero.

Give $N_i=\Sigma_i\times(\mathbb R/B_{3-i}\mathbb Z)$ the product metric with circle term $b^{-1}dy_2^2$. The metrics on $N_i$ have nonpositive sectional curvature. At their remaining boundaries, their formulas agree on full collars with <a href="#eq:end-cometric" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:end-cometric">[eq:end-cometric]</a>, so the torus maps <a href="#eq:end-frames" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:end-frames">[eq:end-frames]</a> attach them smoothly to the neck. The result is a $C^\infty$ Riemannian metric on the closed connected three-manifold specified by $h_2=h_1+f_1$, $f_2=h_1$. Every part outside the open neck, as well as its two indicated endpoint portions, has nonpositive sectional curvature. Choosing the periods after $\delta$ does not affect any estimate on the lifted neck metric.

Figure <a href="#fig:neck-gluing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:neck-gluing">1</a> records the two inward coordinates and shows how the period relation turns the small coordinate shear into the fixed integral gluing.

<figure id="fig:neck-gluing">

<figcaption>The construction in schematic coordinates. Each product piece $N_{i}$ includes its convex collar; the two end portions of the neck also have nonpositive sectional curvature. The arrows indicate the inward coordinate at each end. The lattice cell is drawn in normalized coordinates $(X,Y)$: since $B_{1} = ϵB_{2}$, its diagonal represents $h_{2} = B_{2}(e_{2}+ϵe_{1}) = h_{1} + f_{1}$ even when the coordinate shear $ϵ$ is small. Physical lengths are not to scale, and drawn angles are not angles of the Riemannian metric.</figcaption>
</figure>
